Adding the number of recorded metastatic-site categories changed C by 0.00054 (95% CI -0.00064 to 0.00174; Supplementary Table 26), suggesting that redundancy of data already exists.

When applied to external validation cohort with substantial missingness in input, using labels that are curated with different rules from that of the training set, the model still retains discriminative power. The validation set, which did not require reapplying the evidence enrichment pipeline, reduces a potential source of algorithmspecific validation bias. Minor degradation of model discriminative power (∆ C-index ≤ 0.05) suggests that the model captures transferable prognostic signals. Risk ranking also remained similar under a different endpoint construction, using only the original BPC PFS-I labels, which exclude Mixed imaging assessments (C = 0.643 at DFCI and 0.626 at VICC; Supplementary Table 17). This suggests that the reconstructed labels are sufficiently aligned with independently curated real-world line-level PFS endpoints to support learning prognostic representations that generalize beyond the source cohort. This may be of interest, as different data documentation rules and missingness could be a common theme in medical data.

Across the overall cohort and evaluated subgroups, predicted risk tertiles showed prognostic separation. Internal ICI was 0.91-1.75 percentage points across the evaluated horizons. However, the model overestimated progression in the low-risk tertile, with predicted RMST 10 days below observed overall and 18 days below observed in first lines (Figs. 2b and 4e). After patient-level cross-validated local updates, external ICI was 0.71-1.81 percentage points across the two sites and three horizons. These results support broadly calibrated absolute risk estimates in the evaluated external cohorts after local recalibration. Clinical use still requires evaluation of calibration in the intended population and of the decisions based on those predictions.

Across the overall cohort and within each subtype, GBSA produces risk strata with clear within-group prognostic separation, despite substantial subgroup size imbalance. Line-level attributions describe disease and care-process signals and provide a basis for plausibility checks and hypothesis generation. Several top attributions involve established prognostic domains, including metastatic burden, genomics, and tumor-marker kinetics, and their monotonic directions are biologically sensible (e.g., liver involvement or TP53 alterations should not reduce progression risk). Subtype and initial diagnosis are not among the top contributors, implying that the model relies more on recent disease-burden signals. A plausible explanation is that by mLoT2+, much of the biologic subtype signal has already been "acted upon" through prior regimen selection and earlier outcomes, and the remaining variation in near-term progression risk is increasingly governed by current metastatic burden, marker trajectories, and context-and eligibility-driven treatment choices. Such line-specific attribution shifts describe which signals could be interesting to review at different treatment stages.

Interpretation of some features, however, needs to be put in context. First, the model does not estimate counterfactual treatment effects, and some seeming associations between treatment features and risk are likely confounded. For example, associations such as higher risk with rare chemotherapy or lower risk with targeted/biologic therapy likely reflect confounding by indication and eligibility differences.

Second, missingness in genomic features is associated with lower predicted progression risk. This likely reflects several possible situations, such as patients with genomic data being more likely to have received prior lines, undergone more intensive evaluation, or had more aggressive disease. We retained this signal to represent the absence of genomic data under the line-specific timing rule. In real-world practice, the biological effect of genomic alterations is difficult to separate from the clinical processes that determine whether and when testing occurs, especially given potential time differences between sample acquisition, sequencing, and treatment initiation. Importantly, our ablation results suggest that this proxy does not materially drive performance: removing genomic features caused only negligible performance degradation, while a genomics-only model performed poorly (Fig. 3a). Another example of contextual interpretation is age: higher age is associated with lower predicted risk (Fig. 5a), which is unlikely to be protective biologically. This pattern plausibly reflects cohort composition (older patients enriched for HR-positive and HER2-negative disease; Supplementary Table 1b) and/or more conservative care patterns for older patients. Across the five model families, removing age changed mean outer-fold C by -0.00893 to +0.00024 (Supplementary Tables 9 and 10).

This study has several limitations. First, we model imaging-based PFS under care practices and documentation specific to the source institutions. The modeled population consists of eligible treatment lines under active imaging surveillance at MSK, and generalization to lines without similar follow-up remains uncertain. Prior work has shown that comparability of realworld PFS to trial-based PFS endpoints is limited [27, 28], and that within rwPFS, labels anchored by radiology and clinician can also differ [29]. Our results should be interpreted in light of imaging-based PFS, similar to GENIE BPC's imaging-based labels, rather than as directly interchangeable with clinician-assessed or trial-based PFS. We compare extracted HR+/HER2-PFS on letrozole plus palbociclib, and letrozole against a real-world PFS study [30], since it is the most directly comparable. The study reported weighted clinician-assessed real-world PFS of 20.0 and 11.9 months, with some weighting to balance the study's treatment group. Among first HR+/HER2-metastatic lines, median PFS was 14.9 months with letrozole plus palbociclib and 12.6 months with letrozole alone in our cohort. These medians were 454 and 382 days, compared with 289 and 254 days, respectively, for the same named regimens using endpoint derived from original labels from the GENIE BPC cohort (Supplementary Table 20). GENIE BPC provides a curated external reference for treatment regimens and imaging-based outcomes. Differences in regimen grouping, progression attribution, and censoring conventions should be considered when interpreting reconstruction agreement (Supplementary Table 19).

A line can contain several imaging reports, yet the impact of report-level NLP false positives on line-level PFS remains hard to quantify without access to original reports. Labels are assigned from a report's impression section, but it is not explicitly stated whether reports share a common baseline [17]. Therefore, reports within the same line may not all refer to the patient status at the treatment start, and successive reports may refer to different patient statuses in time. Under rules requiring no subsequent non-progression report within 30, 60 or 90 days, first-line C remained 0.661-0.664, while median PFS increased from 231 days to 274, 311 and 378 days (Supplementary Table 21). Furthermore, independent review in the original MSK-CHORD study attributed 42 of 50 apparent false-positive progression calls to curation errors instead of model error [17]. Although the exact impact remains unquantified, the above two results suggest that annotation accuracy could be reliable, and our central conclusion may not be heavily impacted by the NLP label error rate.

Informative censoring remains a limitation in real-world PFS analyses. In our setting, informative censoring may occur when an mLoT advances to a subsequent line without a qualifying progression label, and is instead censored at the last recorded nonprogression assessment. Although the non-progression status was documented, if the eventual treatment change was related to undocumented disease worsening, outcomes for these lines may differ from those remaining under observation, potentially biasing PFS estimates [31, 32]. This population that could be impacted is bounded above by 674/8,791 mLoTs (7.7%). Because this mechanism can arise only for lines assigned non-progression censoring, and because administrative censoring at 2 years is noninformative by design, only mLoTs censored before the 2-year cutoff may be affected by this bias. Moreover, some of these lines likely reflect non-progression-related treatment changes, such as toxicity, patient preference, or planned escalation, which would not constitute informative censoring. Addressing this limitation will require higherresolution routine-care data and future work on richer data capture and modeling approaches.

A related selection effect arises from the QC rules. For 525 lines (4.7% of the 11,085 eligible lines) in which at least one treatment class first appeared after day 28, the chemotherapy plus endocrine exclusion was not independent of outcome (discussed in detail in Methods, "PFS labeling and censoring"). As a result, the modeled cohort under-represents favorable outcomes in HR-positive lines starting on chemotherapy. We report the original cohort alongside refits that restore these lines. The refits changed overall discrimination little and reduced mean under-prediction in these subgroups, but did not improve external calibration (Supplementary Table 28).

Finally, the model uses treatment exposure recorded within the initial 28-day window and is not established as a prospective day-zero predictor. This constraint follows from the leakage-prevention design discussed above and from the model not updating risk dynamically within a line. Therefore, analyses are strictly retrospective, and prospective clinical validation is needed before the model is used to inform patient care.

There are many directions that future work could pursue. First, mLoT segmentation methods that integrate structured therapy timing with radiology and clinical signals, as well as holistically review multiple radiology labels throughout the trajectory, could reduce reliance on heuristics and improve boundary accuracy. Scalable weak supervision or clinician-in-the-loop audits could further provide training signal and ongoing quality control. Second, adapting dynamic time-series models that update risk within a line and across successive mLoTs at predefined, leakage-safe update times could considerably improve model performance. Third, higher-fidelity inputs (raw imaging, longitudinal labs/vitals, free-text notes, and richer molecular profiling) could improve prognostic resolution, especially in later-line heterogeneous disease. These extensions could broaden applicability beyond curated cohorts and improve prognostic stratification, subject to further validation across diseases and institutions.

Methods

Cohort construction

We included MSK-CHORD patients with breast cancer as the single primary malignancy who had palliative metastatic treatment records and radiology-derived progression assessments. Metastatic onset was approximated as the earliest of (i) the first mention of a distant metastatic site in radiology reports (excluding regional lymph nodes and nonspecific sites) or (ii) the diagnosis date for de novo stage IV disease.

mLoT segmentation

We separately perform mLoT segmentation and PFS labeling to allow for modularity and anchor derived pairs to recorded radiology-based progression assessments. The segmentation algorithm uses systemic treatment records and NLP-annotated radiology progression assessments. For each patient, mLoT1 begins at the first systemic therapy start date after metastatic onset and advances according to the following rules, reflecting clinical conventions and oncologist input.

Importantly, the rules below are used only for mLoT segmentation and never directly label PFS events.

• An mLoT advances after an NLP-labelled radiology progression event that occurs at least 28 days after the current line start date; • An mLoT advances after a new systemic therapy drug (not the preceding drug) that starts at least 1 year after the current line start date; • An mLoT does not advance after local treatment changes (e.g., bone-directed agents, surgery, radiation therapy); • The next mLoT begins at the start date of the first subsequent systemic therapy.

All treatments administered between the start and end of an mLoT are considered part of the mLoT for downstream filtering. An exception applies when a new systemic treatment is initiated within 28 days before the recorded progression that ends the current mLoT. In that case, it is assigned to start a new mLoT to avoid attributing progression to a regimen with insufficient exposure or radiology-assessment latency.

The 28-day threshold is a pragmatic compromise based on clinician input: it allows routine scheduling and assessment latency around line start while avoiding attribution of an observed progression to a regimen with essentially no exposure. Comparable operational conventions for line-of-therapy analyses have also been used in other oncology real-world studies [13, 14, 33, 34]. Sensitivity analyses using narrower and wider linestart treatment windows (14 and 42 days) yielded nearly unchanged discrimination and calibration metrics, supporting robustness of the treatment-window choice rather than dependence on the specific 28-day cutoff (Supplementary Tables 11 and 12).

PFS labeling and censoring

For survival analysis, we label each segmented mLoT with a PFS(event, time) pair. The event label is binary, indicating whether the line ended with progression or death, or was censored. The PFS time is defined as the number of days from line start to the label-defining event, and is not the time between the start of two consecutive mLoTs. We use the following rule set to assign PFS labels: • Progression event: an mLoT is labeled as a progression event if it is terminated by a qualifying NLP-labelled radiology progression event.

The time between line start and the first such progression event is the PFS time; • Death event: for the final mLoT, death without a prior documented progression is also counted as a progression event. The time between line start and death is the PFS time; • Right censoring: if neither of the above occurs, the line is right-censored at the time of the last radiology-confirmed non-progression assessment. PFS time is the number of days from line start to that last non-progression assessment; • Exclusion: lines without any follow-up radiology assessment or death event are not assigned PFS labels and are excluded from modeling.

Radiology-confirmed progression was determined using the progression-status labels provided by MSK-CHORD. We did not develop or re-infer these NLP labels. We treated them as an upstream validated endpoint component, as the original study reported an AUC of 0.97 with precision and recall of 0.90 for progression-status identification [17] against manually curated labels. We additionally apply a pragmatic rule set to further strengthen mLoT regimen and PFS label fidelity. Constructed mLoT-PFS pairs are excluded if: • No radiology assessment or death event occurred during the mLoT; • No radiology assessment occurred within 90 days prior to the start of the mLoT; • The assigned PFS duration is shorter than 28 days; • Both chemotherapy and endocrine therapy were administered within the mLoT; • HR-negative patients received endocrine therapy.

Among 2,294 QC exclusions, 2,114 involve chemotherapy and endocrine therapy within the same mLoT, and 261 involve endocrine therapy in HR-negative disease. Exclusion of lines containing both chemotherapy and endocrine therapy presents an interesting "trilemma". Encoding both classes as simultaneous inputs may lead the model to treat implausible co-prescriptions as normal patterns of care. Encoding the eventual sequence at line start introduces immortal-time bias, since only patients with long PFS go on to receive the later class. Encoding the first class and masking the later class attributes the outcome of the whole line to the drugs given at line start. We used the filter to limit this ambiguity in the treatment representation. Our BPC comparison supports this concern: in all 127 flagged late-start episodes, the later class belonged to a separate BPC regimen (Supplementary Table 28f). However, exclusion has its own cost: for later treatment additions, it selects on remaining progression-free until that treatment is recorded. In 1,589 of the 2,114 flagged lines both classes were recorded within the first 28 days; in 525 (4.7% of the 11,085 eligible lines) the second class was added later. Refitting with the 505 restorable lines, with later agents masked, did not change risk ranking (paired change in C -0.0008, 95% CI -0.0017 to +0.0001). It reduced the under-prediction of one-year PFS in HR-positive first lines starting on chemotherapy (17.0 to 10.5 points in HR+/HER2+; 9.7 to 3.4 in HR+/HER2-) but did not improve external calibration (Supplementary Table 28c-d). Evaluating the saved GBSA models on all 11,085 eligible lines with the 2,294 QC-excluded lines restored left pooled concordance essentially unchanged (C 0.6807 versus 0.6815; C 0.6953 within the QC-excluded lines alone; Supplementary Table 15). The 90-day preline radiology requirement provides a documented baseline disease-state assessment and defines the target population as eligible lines under active imaging surveillance within the institution. This reduces bias from incomplete capture (e.g., outside-system care) that could distort PFS and induce model reliance on non-biological proxies (Supplementary Figure 3). Together, these rule-based filters prevent contradictions with standard-of-care patterns and limit spurious correlations between documentation intensity, subtype labels, and hazard.

We also apply administrative censoring at 730 days (2 years) to mitigate the influence of long-term survivors with limited follow-up and to focus on clinically relevant risk stratification. In the final analytic cohort, 1,226 of 8,791 mLoTs are censored, with 674 lines censored after a documented non-progression assessment and 552 administratively censored at 730 days (Fig. 1b). This design separates mLoT advancement from PFS labeling and makes the progression and censoring rules explicit.

For the first-line endpoint sensitivities, we evaluated rules that required no subsequent non-progression report within 30, 60, or 90 days after a positive report (Supplementary Table 21). For the imaging-eligibility sensitivity, we extended the pre-line imaging requirement from 90 to 180 days and evaluated the saved GBSA models. Performance remained essentially unchanged (pooled C 0.6807 versus 0.6828; Supplementary Table 27).

Feature extraction

Features are extracted and engineered from the MSK-CHORD dataset and are grouped into five modalities: clinicopathologic, radiology, tumor markers, treatment regimen, and genomics (Supplementary Note 1 Feature variables). The model has 205 inputs: 36 treatment, 52 genomic, 36 clinicopathologic, 36 tumor-marker, and 45 radiology inputs (Supplementary Table 18). The feature-extraction pipeline is available in the study code repository. Clinicopathologic features summarize baseline demographics and disease characteristics. Radiology features are derived from radiology reports within 90-day windows prior to line start, capturing tumor presence in specific organs and cancer presence from imaging of different body regions (several regions share the same report outcomes). Tumor-marker features summarize CA15-3 and CEA kinetics using various log-transformed aggregation statistics. Treatment features encode drug agents and local therapies administered within 28 days of line start, using the top five plus an "other" category for each systemic treatment. Genomic features encode somatic mutations and copy-number aberrations in a curated gene list. Genomic features are masked for pre-sequencing lines, with an additional missingness flag for lines that precede sequencing. Under this time-restricted encoding, genomic features are masked for 3,010 of 8,791 modeled mLoTs, spanning 1,805 of 2,881 patients. Additionally, we include a binary flag to indicate whether a line is the first metastatic line, to account for the established prior that treatment benefit tends to diminish across successive lines. Numeric features were centered by the median and scaled by the interquartile range estimated from the training fold, and the same transformation was applied to validation and test data. Missing numeric measurements were then set to zero, which represents the training-fold median. Missing binary indicators were set to zero or encoded with an unknown category, as specified for each feature. Missingness flags, last day of observation, and cancer-presence report status by imaged body region are additionally considered as a "surveillance patterns" modality.

Two design choices specifically mitigate information leakage and non-biological shortcuts. First, drug agents initiated > 28 days after line start are excluded from model inputs. Since mLoTs are constructed retrospectively, encoding the full withinline treatment record risks introducing bias. Longer PFS mechanically permits more post-initiation modifications, such as toxicity switches or drug add-on. As a result, the model may learn a shortcut to associate more drug agents received with longer PFS. The masking is justified by the following observations. mLoTs with agents initiated > 28 days after line start exhibit substantially different PFS distributions from those without late-start agents (Supplementary Figure 4, dashed lines). Models trained without late-start agent masking produced mean predicted curves closer to the corresponding KM estimates and higher apparent discrimination (Supplementary Tables 7 and 8; Supplementary Figure 4).

Second, we applied conservative feature engineering to mitigate potential reliance on surveillance patterns. Because progression is observed through clinical surveillance, models can learn care-process proxies rather than underlying disease risk. For example, given diminishing benefit across successive mLoTs, the model may learn to associate heavier prior treatment with shorter subsequent PFS. We therefore omit explicit treatment trajectories, under the assumption that the current regimen already reflects clinician synthesis of prior response and tolerability. We also suppress finegrained surveillance by omitting measurement counts and timestamps in biomarker kinetics and radiology screening, except for the most recent day of observation and value. Surveillance pattern variables are evaluated in addition to clinical modalities in ablation experiments.

Model selection and training

We benchmarked the following survival models that span varying complexity, adherence to the PH assumption, and interpretability: • (PH, linear) CoxPH [21, 22].

• (PH, tree-based) Gradient-boosted survival model with CoxPH loss (GBSA).

• (Non-PH, tree-based) Random survival forest (RSF) [35].

• (PH, deep learning) DeepSurv [36].

• (Non-PH, deep learning) DeepHit [37].

Code for model implementations and evaluation metrics is adapted from the framework and Python notebooks in [38].

We performed nested cross-validation (nested CV) with 5 outer folds for evaluation and 3 inner folds for hyperparameter selection. This provides an unbiased estimate of held-out performance under hyperparameter exploration and model selection. For GBSA, RSF, DeepSurv, and DeepHit, we evaluated five preselected configurations (Supplementary Note 2 Model parameters) spanning low-, medium-, and high-capacity models with different regularization strengths. For CoxPH, we tuned the L2 regularization strength α over 30 log-spaced values between 10 -1 and 10 3 . Best-performing configuration candidates in each outer fold were those with average inner-fold C-index within 0.003 of the best-performing configuration. The final configuration is selected in favor of lower-complexity and more strongly regularized configurations. CV splits were stratified by HR/HER2 subtype and whether a patient had at least one mLoT censored at 2 years. All mLoTs from a given patient were assigned to the same split to prevent leakage. Detailed candidate grids are provided in Supplementary Note 2 Model parameters, and selected outer-fold configurations are summarized in Supplementary Table 14. Ablation experiments use the selected best configuration, restricted to configurations selected at least once in the full-modality nested CV to reduce computational burden.

The clinical Cox reference used first-line status, HR and HER2 status, the most recent ECOG value, liver involvement in the preceding 90 days, and the most recent CA15-3 value within 180 days before line start, transformed as log(1 + x), with α = 1 (Supplementary Table 23). Additional GBSA analyses jointly removed first-line status and all missingness indicators, or added the number of recorded metastatic-site categories in the preceding 90 days (Supplementary Tables 24 and 26).

Evaluation

We harmonized each model's output to a survival probability curve on a common time grid over 2 years. Global discrimination metrics are Antolini's C [39] and cumulative/dynamic AUC (cdAUC) [40]. Antolini's C considers all comparable pairs of mLoTs over follow-up and reports the fraction for which the line with the shorter observed PFS receives higher predicted risk. cdAUC is computed at each time t by forming the time-specific risk set, pairing individuals who experience an event prior to time t with those who remain event-free past t, and estimating the probability that the model assigns higher risk to the former; inverse-probability weights correct for censoring. An average over the evaluation time range yields the reported single overall discrimination score. For time-dependent evaluation, we report IPCW C-index [41], cdAUC, and Brier score [42] at 30-day increments. Inverse-probability-of-censoring weights were estimated with the Kaplan-Meier method in the population being evaluated: the MSK-CHORD training folds for internal evaluation and each external site's own regimens for external evaluation. To prevent degenerate inverse-probability-ofcensoring weights at the administrative-censoring boundary, any PFS event occurring exactly at 730 d is shifted to 729.999 d (i.e., 730-0.001). This negligible adjustment avoids censoring-survival estimates reaching 0 at the evaluation horizon and stabilizes IPCW-based metrics. For the same reason, IPCW-based metrics at the 2-year horizon are evaluated at 730-ε rather than exactly 730 d. When a nominal horizon (e.g., 730 d) is not exactly represented on a model's evaluation grid, we evaluate at the closest available time point and report it at the nominal horizon.

Pooled out-of-fold estimates were reported with percentile 95% confidence intervals from 1,000 patient-level bootstrap resamples, retaining all lines of each sampled patient. Model differences used paired patient draws. Predictions remained fixed, so these intervals describe evaluation-sample uncertainty and do not include uncertainty from model training.

Calibration was evaluated at 180, 365, and 730 days using IPCW, with ICI calculated from a three-knot natural cubic-spline calibration curve and reported in percentage points. Calibration-in-the-large is the intercept from an IPCW logistic model with the coefficient of predicted log-odds fixed at one; calibration slope is estimated with a free intercept. Their ideal values are zero and one, respectively; positive calibration-in-the-large indicates average under-estimation of progression risk, and a slope above one indicates compressed predictions. ICI, E50, and E90 are the mean, median, and 90th percentile of absolute calibration error, respectively. Internal probabilities are out-of-fold, whereas external probabilities are averaged across the five fixed fold models. Calibration intervals use 1,000 patient resamples, retaining all lines of each sampled patient; censoring estimates and calibration models are refitted, while prediction models and spline knots remain fixed. Additional calibration measures-calibration-in-the-large, calibration slope, E50, and E90-are reported in Supplementary Table 25. The completed TRIPOD+AI checklist is provided in Supplementary Note 3.

External recalibration

At each external site and at 180, 365, and 730 days, we fitted an IPCW-weighted logistic model to estimate an intercept and slope for the log-odds of the mean predicted progression probability from the five fixed GBSA models. The updated log-odds equalled the intercept plus the slope multiplied by the original log-odds. We evaluated the update using five patient-level folds within each site; each regimen received an update fitted on patients in the other four folds. Recalibration intervals used 1,000 patient-level bootstrap resamples with the updated probabilities held fixed; the update was not refitted within each resample. For the pooled external ICI summary, we combined the DFCI and VICC regimens and fitted the same calibration diagnostic with pooled censoring weights to the original or site-specific cross-validated updated probabilities. This pooled estimate is distinct from the site-specific results. Continuous calibration curves and observed versus predicted survival within external risk tertiles are shown in Supplementary Figures 5 6.

Reconstruction comparison on GENIE BPC

We applied the mLoT segmentation and PFS-labeling rules to curated GENIE BPC treatment and imaging records at DFCI, VICC, and MSK. A reconstructed episode and a BPC regimen were matched within the same patient if they had identical drug sets after drug-name normalization and removal of GnRH agonists (since GENIE BPC counts these as separate regimens), with start dates at most 14 days apart. We reported regimen matching among retained reconstructed episodes and event-indicator and exact-duration agreement among matched pairs. Listed grouping and endpoint-rule differences were reported separately from exact agreement (Supplementary Table 19).

External validation on the AACR Project GENIE BPC cohort

In external validation, the five GBSA models trained on the MSK-CHORD outer folds were applied unchanged. We used curated GENIE BPC metastatic regimen boundaries, which follow BPC grouping rules and are not equivalent to reconstructed MSK-CHORD mLoTs. We derived PFS-I + Mixed from curated imaging assessments, counting the first Progressing or Mixed report after regimen start, or death, as an event; censoring follows the BPC next-regimen and last-contact rules [20]. We included Mixed assessments as progression in BPC PFS-I to match the positive class of the MSK-CHORD radiology labels. We retained the original regimen and drug eligibility rules, reapplied the minimum 28-day PFS duration to the revised endpoint, and administratively censored at 730 days. The main analysis uses this endpoint; Supplementary Table 17 also retains the released PFS-I analysis for comparison.

Not all model inputs are available in GENIE BPC. ECOG performance status, CEA, MMR status, and surgery and radiation are not released; these inputs were set to missing for every regimen, as for an MSK-CHORD line without that measurement. CA15-3 was recorded for 20.8% of DFCI and 3.7% of VICC regimens, and PD-L1 status was unknown for nearly all regimens. Radiology-report mentions of tumor sites are not released; the metastatic-site inputs were instead derived from the curated dates of first distant metastasis at each site. All other inputs were built from curated BPC fields.

Risk stratification and Kaplan-Meier summaries

Risk stratification and interpretability analyses are performed on out-of-fold predictions from the primary model (GBSA). mLoTs are stratified into low-, mid-, and high-risk tertiles using the predicted 1-year progression probability, and we compare the mean predicted survival curve to the Kaplan-Meier estimate within each stratum. Risk tertile cutoffs and SHAP contrasts were calculated at line level. For proportionalhazards models, the risk ranking is time-invariant and thus the same tertile cutoffs apply across time horizons. We summarize the discrepancy using the mean absolute error (MAE) between predicted and observed survival curves over time; a low MAE means the predicted survival probabilities differ from the corresponding Kaplan-Meier estimate by only a small amount on average. We additionally report RMST at 365 days, which informs the expected time free of progression or death within the first year of treatment.

SHAP contributions

We interpret GBSA predictions at the feature-group level using SHAP values computed from SHAP's PermutationExplainer on the predicted PFS probability at 365 days. Although models trained on different folds may learn different baseline hazards and risk-score calibrations, the predicted PFS probability at a fixed time point is directly comparable across folds. To intuitively correlate higher SHAP output values with higher progression risk, we report SHAP contributions for 1 -PFS(365) (i.e., progression probability by 365 days).

Input-feature value contrasts

Let x ij denote the value of input feature j for line i under the feature windows defined above. To report subtype-(or mLoT-) specific contrasts relative to the cohort baseline, we re-center each input feature within cohort G,

We then quantify separation between risk strata using Cohen's d computed on xij within cohort G:

Positive d (x) j (G) indicates higher mean feature values in the high-risk tertile, while negative values indicate higher mean feature values in the low-risk tertile. We apply this same input-feature value contrast within each HR/HER2 subtype and within each mLoT stratum (mLoT1 and mLoT2+).

SHAP-based contrasts at the group level

Let ϕ ij denote the SHAP attribution for feature j on line i for the prediction 1 -PFS i (365). We additionally map model inputs into clinically motivated feature groups (Supplementary Table 16) and summarize each group's contribution using a per-line relative-importance (share) score, where g denotes a feature group and the denominator sums absolute SHAP magnitudes across groups.

This normalization yields the fraction of total absolute attribution assigned to group g for line i and avoids sign cancellation across groups. We compare these group shares directly between risk strata within cohort G (without re-centering), indicates that group g accounts for a larger share of total absolute attribution in the high-risk tertile; negative values indicate a larger share in the lowrisk tertile.

Cohen's d and pooled variance

For any scalar quantity z(i) compared between H and L within cohort G (e.g., xij or s g (i)), we compute Cohen's d as where zH and zL are means in the high-and low-risk tertiles, s 2 H and s 2 L are their sample variances, and n H , n L are the tertile sizes.

Throughout, these attributions and contrasts are descriptive and non-causal in this observational setting.
